Um… I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I’m not really someone who goes to the gym for hours or anything like that. Sometimes after it happens I feel really restless and uncomfortable in my body, and I might think like, “I should move around” or “I need to do something,” but I don’t have, like, a routine where I’m trying to burn it all off.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean… if it’s actually one of those episodes, then afterward I pretty much always feel bad about it. That’s kind of the whole reason I finally came in, honestly, because it’s not just eating too much and moving on. It’s like this heavy, gross feeling afterward, like I’m disgusted with myself or really disappointed in myself.

I’ll sit there thinking, “Why couldn’t you just stop?” or “Why don’t you have more control?” And then sometimes I write about it in my journal, but it doesn’t come out helpful or reflective, it comes out really mean. The shame part is usually right there with it.

It’s not exactly once a week, no. I guess when I say “at least once,” I mean there’s usually one night every week where it happens, but some weeks it can be more than that. The pattern I notice most is after work — I’ll stop somewhere or order food, and I end up buying way more than I meant to, and then I eat it all in one stretch once I’m home.

It’s been like that for the past several months. I’m trying to think if there was a stretch where it really stopped, and… I honestly can’t remember a recent month where I went more than maybe a week or so without it happening. So it’s not like a perfectly scheduled thing, like every Tuesday or something, but it’s frequent enough that it feels like it’s always coming back.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.4, F50.5\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I came in because I’ve been having these eating episodes, and what really scares me is how I feel afterward. Like, after it happens, I just feel so gross and ashamed, and really disappointed in myself. I keep thinking, “Why can’t I just have more control?” and then I sort of spiral.\n\nI’ve been writing in my journal afterward, which I thought would help, but honestly it’s turned into me writing really awful things about myself. Like I’m just sitting there tearing myself apart on paper. And I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels… I don’t know, like proof that something’s wrong with me. I don’t want to keep feeling like that after I eat.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt really sick and uncomfortable afterward, like physically gross and way too full, but I haven’t made myself throw up. I’ve had moments where I wished I could just undo it somehow, if that makes sense, but I don’t go and vomit on purpose. It’s more like I just sit there feeling awful about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to try to burn off calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I’m not really someone who goes to the gym for hours or anything like that. Sometimes after it happens I feel really restless and uncomfortable in my body, and I might think like, “I should move around” or “I need to do something,” but I don’t have, like, a routine where I’m trying to burn it all off.\n\nI do clean for work, so I’m on my feet a lot anyway, and sometimes I’ll sort of tell myself that being busy or moving around is better than just sitting there feeling disgusting, but I don’t think I’m exercising on purpose to cancel out what I ate. It’s more the guilt and the feeling bad about myself that sits there afterward.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s been pretty regular, I guess. For the past several months, it’s usually at least once a week. It tends to happen at night, after work. I’ll stop and buy way too much food, more than I meant to, and then I just eat it all in one stretch.\n\nI keep thinking maybe it was just a bad week or a stress thing, but when I look back, I can’t really remember a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. Like, it might skip a week, but then it comes back again. I don’t weigh myself regularly because it kind of makes me spiral, so I don’t have a number to give you. It’s more that I feel uncomfortable in my body and then I start checking or avoiding checking, if that makes sense. So… I don’t really know if my weight has changed significantly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to undo the effects of one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I mean, I know what you mean, like taking something to make yourself go to the bathroom or lose water weight or whatever, but that’s not something I do after those episodes.\n\nI’ve maybe had the thought in a really panicky way, like, “How do I make this not count?” but I don’t go buy laxatives or take water pills or anything like that. Honestly, I’d be kind of scared to mess with that stuff. Afterward I mostly just feel gross and guilty and try to get through it, but I’m not using anything like that to undo it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food in a short period, but you don't feel guilty or ashamed about it afterward?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… if it’s actually one of those episodes, then afterward I pretty much always feel bad about it. Like I’ll write awful things about myself that I wouldn’t say to another person. So… no, I don’t think there are many times where it happens and I feel fine afterward. I guess when I say “at least once,” I mean there’s usually one night every week where it happens, but some weeks it can be more than that. The pattern I notice most is after work — I’ll stop somewhere or order food, and I end up buying way more than I meant to, and then I eat it all in one stretch once I’m home.\n\nIt’s been like that for the past several months. I’m trying to think if there was a stretch where it really stopped, and… I honestly can’t remember a recent month where I went more than maybe a week or so without it happening. So it’s not like a perfectly scheduled thing, like every Tuesday or something, but it’s frequent enough that it feels like it’s always coming back.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… there are some weeks where it’s more than twice, but I don’t think that’s the usual pattern. Usually it’s more like there’s at least one night in the week where I get off work and I’m already kind of in that mode, and I stop for food or order too much, and then once I’m home I eat it all in one stretch.

But it’s not like I can say, “Oh, it’s exactly once every week,” because some weeks it does happen more. I’m trying to be accurate, because I don’t want to exaggerate it, but I also don’t want to make it sound smaller than it is. For the past several months, I really can’t think of a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. It keeps coming back, even when I tell myself I’m not going to do it again.

It’s more the guilt and the feeling bad about myself that sits there afterward.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s been pretty regular, I guess. For the past several months, it’s usually at least once a week. It tends to happen at night, after work. I’ll stop and buy way too much food, more than I meant to, and then I just eat it all in one stretch.\n\nI keep thinking maybe it was just a bad week or a stress thing, but when I look back, I can’t really remember a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. Like, it might skip a week, but then it comes back again. So… yeah, usually weekly, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t weigh myself regularly because it kind of makes me spiral, so I don’t have a number to give you. My clothes maybe feel different sometimes, but then other days I convince myself I’m just bloated or I’m overthinking it.\n\nI haven’t had, like, a dramatic change that I can point to for sure. It’s more that I feel uncomfortable in my body and then I start checking or avoiding checking, if that makes sense. So… I don’t really know if my weight has changed significantly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to undo the effects of one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I mean, I know what you mean, like taking something to make yourself go to the bathroom or lose water weight or whatever, but that’s not something I do after those episodes.\n\nI’ve maybe had the thought in a really panicky way, like, “How do I make this not count?” but I don’t go buy laxatives or take water pills or anything like that. Honestly, I’d be kind of scared to mess with that stuff. Afterward I mostly just feel gross and guilty and try to get through it, but I’m not using anything like that to undo it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food in a short period, but you don't feel guilty or ashamed about it afterward?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… if it’s actually one of those episodes, then afterward I pretty much always feel bad about it. That’s kind of the whole reason I finally came in, honestly, because it’s not just eating too much and moving on. It’s like this heavy, gross feeling afterward, like I’m disgusted with myself or really disappointed in myself.\n\nI’ll sit there thinking, “Why couldn’t you just stop?” or “Why don’t you have more control?” And then sometimes I write about it in my journal, but it doesn’t come out helpful or reflective, it comes out really mean. Like I’ll write awful things about myself that I wouldn’t say to another person. So… no, I don’t think there are many times where it happens and I feel fine afterward. The shame part is usually right there with it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by 'at least once a week', is it exactly once a week or are there weeks where it happens more than once?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly once a week, no. I guess when I say “at least once,” I mean there’s usually one night every week where it happens, but some weeks it can be more than that. The pattern I notice most is after work — I’ll stop somewhere or order food, and I end up buying way more than I meant to, and then I eat it all in one stretch once I’m home.\n\nIt’s been like that for the past several months. I’m trying to think if there was a stretch where it really stopped, and… I honestly can’t remember a recent month where I went more than maybe a week or so without it happening. So it’s not like a perfectly scheduled thing, like every Tuesday or something, but it’s frequent enough that it feels like it’s always coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you have weeks where these eating episodes happen more than twice?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are some weeks where it’s more than twice, but I don’t think that’s the usual pattern. Usually it’s more like there’s at least one night in the week where I get off work and I’m already kind of in that mode, and I stop for food or order too much, and then once I’m home I eat it all in one stretch.\n\nBut it’s not like I can say, “Oh, it’s exactly once every week,” because some weeks it does happen more. I’m trying to be accurate, because I don’t want to exaggerate it, but I also don’t want to make it sound smaller than it is. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that’s probably the clearest part of it, honestly. Once I start, it feels like I’m not really making decisions one by one anymore. It’s like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically, even if part of my brain is still talking at me the whole time.

Like I’ll literally be thinking, “Fumi, stop. You have sketches to finish. You said you were going to work on them tonight.” Or I’ll think, “Just put the rest away, you don’t need more.” But then I don’t actually do that. I just keep going. It’s not even always that the food tastes amazing by that point, either. Sometimes I’m already not enjoying it, but I still keep eating until it’s gone, or until I feel so uncomfortable that stopping kind of happens because I physically don’t want to keep going.

So yes, it does feel like I can’t stop once I’ve started. Or maybe… I know technically I could stop, but in the moment it really doesn’t feel like I can.

I’ll stop and buy way too much food, more than I meant to, and then I just eat it all in one stretch.\n\nI keep thinking maybe it was just a bad week or a stress thing, but when I look back, I can’t really remember a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. Like, it might skip a week, but then it comes back again. So… yeah, usually weekly, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t weigh myself regularly because it kind of makes me spiral, so I don’t have a number to give you. My clothes maybe feel different sometimes, but then other days I convince myself I’m just bloated or I’m overthinking it.\n\nI haven’t had, like, a dramatic change that I can point to for sure. It’s more that I feel uncomfortable in my body and then I start checking or avoiding checking, if that makes sense. So… I don’t really know if my weight has changed significantly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to undo the effects of one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I mean, I know what you mean, like taking something to make yourself go to the bathroom or lose water weight or whatever, but that’s not something I do after those episodes.\n\nI’ve maybe had the thought in a really panicky way, like, “How do I make this not count?” but I don’t go buy laxatives or take water pills or anything like that. Honestly, I’d be kind of scared to mess with that stuff. Afterward I mostly just feel gross and guilty and try to get through it, but I’m not using anything like that to undo it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food in a short period, but you don't feel guilty or ashamed about it afterward?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… if it’s actually one of those episodes, then afterward I pretty much always feel bad about it. That’s kind of the whole reason I finally came in, honestly, because it’s not just eating too much and moving on. It’s like this heavy, gross feeling afterward, like I’m disgusted with myself or really disappointed in myself.\n\nI’ll sit there thinking, “Why couldn’t you just stop?” or “Why don’t you have more control?” And then sometimes I write about it in my journal, but it doesn’t come out helpful or reflective, it comes out really mean. Like I’ll write awful things about myself that I wouldn’t say to another person. So… no, I don’t think there are many times where it happens and I feel fine afterward. The shame part is usually right there with it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by 'at least once a week', is it exactly once a week or are there weeks where it happens more than once?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly once a week, no. I guess when I say “at least once,” I mean there’s usually one night every week where it happens, but some weeks it can be more than that. The pattern I notice most is after work — I’ll stop somewhere or order food, and I end up buying way more than I meant to, and then I eat it all in one stretch once I’m home.\n\nIt’s been like that for the past several months. I’m trying to think if there was a stretch where it really stopped, and… I honestly can’t remember a recent month where I went more than maybe a week or so without it happening. So it’s not like a perfectly scheduled thing, like every Tuesday or something, but it’s frequent enough that it feels like it’s always coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you have weeks where these eating episodes happen more than twice?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are some weeks where it’s more than twice, but I don’t think that’s the usual pattern. Usually it’s more like there’s at least one night in the week where I get off work and I’m already kind of in that mode, and I stop for food or order too much, and then once I’m home I eat it all in one stretch.\n\nBut it’s not like I can say, “Oh, it’s exactly once every week,” because some weeks it does happen more. I’m trying to be accurate, because I don’t want to exaggerate it, but I also don’t want to make it sound smaller than it is. For the past several months, I really can’t think of a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. It keeps coming back, even when I tell myself I’m not going to do it again.\n\nDoctor: During these eating episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the clearest part of it, honestly. Once I start, it feels like I’m not really making decisions one by one anymore. It’s like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically, even if part of my brain is still talking at me the whole time.\n\nLike I’ll literally be thinking, “Fumi, stop. You have sketches to finish. You said you were going to work on them tonight.” Or I’ll think, “Just put the rest away, you don’t need more.” But then I don’t actually do that. I just keep going. It’s not even always that the food tastes amazing by that point, either. Sometimes I’m already not enjoying it, but I still keep eating until it’s gone, or until I feel so uncomfortable that stopping kind of happens because I physically don’t want to keep going.\n\nSo yes, it does feel like I can’t stop once I’ve started. Or maybe… I know technically I could stop, but in the moment it really doesn’t feel like I can.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I came in because I’ve been having these eating episodes, and what really scares me is how I feel afterward. Like, after it happens, I just feel so gross and ashamed, and really disappointed in myself. I keep thinking, “Why can’t I just have more control?” and then I sort of spiral.\n\nI’ve been writing in my journal afterward, which I thought would help, but honestly it’s turned into me writing really awful things about myself. Like I’m just sitting there tearing myself apart on paper. And I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels… I don’t know, like proof that something’s wrong with me. I don’t want to keep feeling like that after I eat.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt really sick and uncomfortable afterward, like physically gross and way too full, but I haven’t made myself throw up. I’ve had moments where I wished I could just undo it somehow, if that makes sense, but I don’t go and vomit on purpose. It’s more like I just sit there feeling awful about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to try to burn off calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I’m not really someone who goes to the gym for hours or anything like that. Sometimes after it happens I feel really restless and uncomfortable in my body, and I might think like, “I should move around” or “I need to do something,” but I don’t have, like, a routine where I’m trying to burn it all off.\n\nI do clean for work, so I’m on my feet a lot anyway, and sometimes I’ll sort of tell myself that being busy or moving around is better than just sitting there feeling disgusting, but I don’t think I’m exercising on purpose to cancel out what I ate. It’s more the guilt and the feeling bad about myself that sits there afterward.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s been pretty regular, I guess. For the past several months, it’s usually at least once a week. It tends to happen at night, after work. I’ll stop and buy way too much food, more than I meant to, and then I just eat it all in one stretch.\n\nI keep thinking maybe it was just a bad week or a stress thing, but when I look back, I can’t really remember a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. Like, it might skip a week, but then it comes back again. So… yeah, usually weekly, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t weigh myself regularly because it kind of makes me spiral, so I don’t have a number to give you. My clothes maybe feel different sometimes, but then other days I convince myself I’m just bloated or I’m overthinking it.\n\nI haven’t had, like, a dramatic change that I can point to for sure. It’s more that I feel uncomfortable in my body and then I start checking or avoiding checking, if that makes sense. So… I don’t really know if my weight has changed significantly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to undo the effects of one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I mean, I know what you mean, like taking something to make yourself go to the bathroom or lose water weight or whatever, but that’s not something I do after those episodes.\n\nI’ve maybe had the thought in a really panicky way, like, “How do I make this not count?” but I don’t go buy laxatives or take water pills or anything like that. Honestly, I’d be kind of scared to mess with that stuff. Afterward I mostly just feel gross and guilty and try to get through it, but I’m not using anything like that to undo it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food in a short period, but you don't feel guilty or ashamed about it afterward?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… if it’s actually one of those episodes, then afterward I pretty much always feel bad about it. That’s kind of the whole reason I finally came in, honestly, because it’s not just eating too much and moving on. It’s like this heavy, gross feeling afterward, like I’m disgusted with myself or really disappointed in myself.\n\nI’ll sit there thinking, “Why couldn’t you just stop?” or “Why don’t you have more control?” And then sometimes I write about it in my journal, but it doesn’t come out helpful or reflective, it comes out really mean. Like I’ll write awful things about myself that I wouldn’t say to another person. So… no, I don’t think there are many times where it happens and I feel fine afterward. The shame part is usually right there with it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by 'at least once a week', is it exactly once a week or are there weeks where it happens more than once?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly once a week, no. I guess when I say “at least once,” I mean there’s usually one night every week where it happens, but some weeks it can be more than that. The pattern I notice most is after work — I’ll stop somewhere or order food, and I end up buying way more than I meant to, and then I eat it all in one stretch once I’m home.\n\nIt’s been like that for the past several months. I’m trying to think if there was a stretch where it really stopped, and… I honestly can’t remember a recent month where I went more than maybe a week or so without it happening. So it’s not like a perfectly scheduled thing, like every Tuesday or something, but it’s frequent enough that it feels like it’s always coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you have weeks where these eating episodes happen more than twice?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are some weeks where it’s more than twice, but I don’t think that’s the usual pattern. Usually it’s more like there’s at least one night in the week where I get off work and I’m already kind of in that mode, and I stop for food or order too much, and then once I’m home I eat it all in one stretch.\n\nBut it’s not like I can say, “Oh, it’s exactly once every week,” because some weeks it does happen more. I’m trying to be accurate, because I don’t want to exaggerate it, but I also don’t want to make it sound smaller than it is. For the past several months, I really can’t think of a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. It keeps coming back, even when I tell myself I’m not going to do it again.\n\nDoctor: During these eating episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the clearest part of it, honestly. Once I start, it feels like I’m not really making decisions one by one anymore. It’s like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically, even if part of my brain is still talking at me the whole time.\n\nLike I’ll literally be thinking, “Fumi, stop. You have sketches to finish. You said you were going to work on them tonight.” Or I’ll think, “Just put the rest away, you don’t need more.” But then I don’t actually do that. I just keep going. It’s not even always that the food tastes amazing by that point, either. Sometimes I’m already not enjoying it, but I still keep eating until it’s gone, or until I feel so uncomfortable that stopping kind of happens because I physically don’t want to keep going.\n\nSo yes, it does feel like I can’t stop once I’ve started. Or maybe… I know technically I could stop, but in the moment it really doesn’t feel like I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself or feel guilty about things unrelated to your eating?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I do criticize myself sometimes, like anyone does, I guess — if I mess something up at work or if I procrastinate on something I meant to do. But I don’t know if I’d say it’s a big thing outside of the eating stuff.\n\nThe guilt is mostly tied to that, at least that’s what stands out to me. Like afterward I can get really harsh with myself. But unrelated to eating… I’d have to think about it more. Nothing is immediately coming to mind where I’m constantly feeling guilty about everything, if that’s what you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F50.4", "F50.8"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not often, at least. I mean, I can be hard on myself in a normal way, like if I’m late getting something done or I feel like I handled something awkwardly, I’ll replay it a bit. But when you say “very critical,” I think of that really intense kind of beating myself up, and that’s much more connected to the eating for me.

Outside of food, I don’t walk around constantly thinking I’m a terrible person or anything like that. I might get annoyed with myself or embarrassed, but it usually passes. So… no, I wouldn’t say I often do that in situations that have nothing to do with food.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.4, F50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I came in because I’ve been having these eating episodes, and what really scares me is how I feel afterward. Like, after it happens, I just feel so gross and ashamed, and really disappointed in myself. I keep thinking, “Why can’t I just have more control?” and then I sort of spiral.\n\nI’ve been writing in my journal afterward, which I thought would help, but honestly it’s turned into me writing really awful things about myself. Like I’m just sitting there tearing myself apart on paper. And I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels… I don’t know, like proof that something’s wrong with me. I don’t want to keep feeling like that after I eat.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt really sick and uncomfortable afterward, like physically gross and way too full, but I haven’t made myself throw up. I’ve had moments where I wished I could just undo it somehow, if that makes sense, but I don’t go and vomit on purpose. It’s more like I just sit there feeling awful about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to try to burn off calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I’m not really someone who goes to the gym for hours or anything like that. Sometimes after it happens I feel really restless and uncomfortable in my body, and I might think like, “I should move around” or “I need to do something,” but I don’t have, like, a routine where I’m trying to burn it all off.\n\nI do clean for work, so I’m on my feet a lot anyway, and sometimes I’ll sort of tell myself that being busy or moving around is better than just sitting there feeling disgusting, but I don’t think I’m exercising on purpose to cancel out what I ate. It’s more the guilt and the feeling bad about myself that sits there afterward.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s been pretty regular, I guess. For the past several months, it’s usually at least once a week. It tends to happen at night, after work. I’ll stop and buy way too much food, more than I meant to, and then I just eat it all in one stretch.\n\nI keep thinking maybe it was just a bad week or a stress thing, but when I look back, I can’t really remember a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. Like, it might skip a week, but then it comes back again. So… yeah, usually weekly, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t weigh myself regularly because it kind of makes me spiral, so I don’t have a number to give you. My clothes maybe feel different sometimes, but then other days I convince myself I’m just bloated or I’m overthinking it.\n\nI haven’t had, like, a dramatic change that I can point to for sure. It’s more that I feel uncomfortable in my body and then I start checking or avoiding checking, if that makes sense. So… I don’t really know if my weight has changed significantly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to undo the effects of one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I mean, I know what you mean, like taking something to make yourself go to the bathroom or lose water weight or whatever, but that’s not something I do after those episodes.\n\nI’ve maybe had the thought in a really panicky way, like, “How do I make this not count?” but I don’t go buy laxatives or take water pills or anything like that. Honestly, I’d be kind of scared to mess with that stuff. Afterward I mostly just feel gross and guilty and try to get through it, but I’m not using anything like that to undo it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food in a short period, but you don't feel guilty or ashamed about it afterward?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… if it’s actually one of those episodes, then afterward I pretty much always feel bad about it. That’s kind of the whole reason I finally came in, honestly, because it’s not just eating too much and moving on. It’s like this heavy, gross feeling afterward, like I’m disgusted with myself or really disappointed in myself.\n\nI’ll sit there thinking, “Why couldn’t you just stop?” or “Why don’t you have more control?” And then sometimes I write about it in my journal, but it doesn’t come out helpful or reflective, it comes out really mean. Like I’ll write awful things about myself that I wouldn’t say to another person. So… no, I don’t think there are many times where it happens and I feel fine afterward. The shame part is usually right there with it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by 'at least once a week', is it exactly once a week or are there weeks where it happens more than once?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly once a week, no. I guess when I say “at least once,” I mean there’s usually one night every week where it happens, but some weeks it can be more than that. The pattern I notice most is after work — I’ll stop somewhere or order food, and I end up buying way more than I meant to, and then I eat it all in one stretch once I’m home.\n\nIt’s been like that for the past several months. I’m trying to think if there was a stretch where it really stopped, and… I honestly can’t remember a recent month where I went more than maybe a week or so without it happening. So it’s not like a perfectly scheduled thing, like every Tuesday or something, but it’s frequent enough that it feels like it’s always coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you have weeks where these eating episodes happen more than twice?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are some weeks where it’s more than twice, but I don’t think that’s the usual pattern. Usually it’s more like there’s at least one night in the week where I get off work and I’m already kind of in that mode, and I stop for food or order too much, and then once I’m home I eat it all in one stretch.\n\nBut it’s not like I can say, “Oh, it’s exactly once every week,” because some weeks it does happen more. I’m trying to be accurate, because I don’t want to exaggerate it, but I also don’t want to make it sound smaller than it is. For the past several months, I really can’t think of a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. It keeps coming back, even when I tell myself I’m not going to do it again.\n\nDoctor: During these eating episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the clearest part of it, honestly. Once I start, it feels like I’m not really making decisions one by one anymore. It’s like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically, even if part of my brain is still talking at me the whole time.\n\nLike I’ll literally be thinking, “Fumi, stop. You have sketches to finish. You said you were going to work on them tonight.” Or I’ll think, “Just put the rest away, you don’t need more.” But then I don’t actually do that. I just keep going. It’s not even always that the food tastes amazing by that point, either. Sometimes I’m already not enjoying it, but I still keep eating until it’s gone, or until I feel so uncomfortable that stopping kind of happens because I physically don’t want to keep going.\n\nSo yes, it does feel like I can’t stop once I’ve started. Or maybe… I know technically I could stop, but in the moment it really doesn’t feel like I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself or feel guilty about things unrelated to your eating?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I do criticize myself sometimes, like anyone does, I guess — if I mess something up at work or if I procrastinate on something I meant to do. But I don’t know if I’d say it’s a big thing outside of the eating stuff.\n\nThe guilt is mostly tied to that, at least that’s what stands out to me. Like afterward I can get really harsh with myself. But unrelated to eating… I’d have to think about it more. Nothing is immediately coming to mind where I’m constantly feeling guilty about everything, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being very critical of yourself in situations that have nothing to do with food or eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not often, at least. I mean, I can be hard on myself in a normal way, like if I’m late getting something done or I feel like I handled something awkwardly, I’ll replay it a bit. But when you say “very critical,” I think of that really intense kind of beating myself up, and that’s much more connected to the eating for me.\n\nOutside of food, I don’t walk around constantly thinking I’m a terrible person or anything like that. I might get annoyed with myself or embarrassed, but it usually passes. So… no, I wouldn’t say I often do that in situations that have nothing to do with food.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: During these eating episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the clearest part of it, honestly. Once I start, it feels like I’m not really making decisions one by one anymore. It’s like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically, even if part of my brain is still talking at me the whole time.\n\nLike I’ll literally be thinking, “Fumi, stop. You have sketches to finish. You said you were going to work on them tonight.” Or I’ll think, “Just put the rest away, you don’t need more.” But then I don’t actually do that. I just keep going. Sometimes I’m already not enjoying it, but I still keep eating until it’s gone, or until I feel so uncomfortable that stopping kind of happens because I physically don’t want to keep going.\n\nSo yes, it does feel like I can’t stop once I’ve started. Or maybe… I know technically I could stop, but in the moment it really doesn’t feel like I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself or feel guilty about things unrelated to your eating?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. Nothing is immediately coming to mind where I’m constantly feeling guilty about everything, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being very critical of yourself in situations that have nothing to do with food or eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not often, at least. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s mostly after the eating episodes. That’s really the part that made me come in, honestly, because afterward I just feel… gross, and disappointed in myself, like I should have had more control or I should have stopped earlier. It feels very personal, like it turns into “what is wrong with me?” instead of just “I ate too much.”

And I’ll write about it in my journal, which sounds like it should help, but sometimes it’s just awful. I’ll write really harsh things about myself, things I probably wouldn’t say to another person. Like I’ll sit there feeling sick and embarrassed and write pages of how I messed up again and how I should be better than this.

In other areas of my life, I can criticize myself, but it doesn’t usually get that intense or mean. It’s really after those episodes that it kind of spirals into that ugly self-talk.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.4, F50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I came in because I’ve been having these eating episodes, and what really scares me is how I feel afterward. Like, after it happens, I just feel so gross and ashamed, and really disappointed in myself. I keep thinking, “Why can’t I just have more control?” and then I sort of spiral.\n\nI’ve been writing in my journal afterward, which I thought would help, but honestly it’s turned into me writing really awful things about myself. Like I’m just sitting there tearing myself apart on paper. And I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels… I don’t know, like proof that something’s wrong with me. I don’t want to keep feeling like that after I eat.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt really sick and uncomfortable afterward, like physically gross and way too full, but I haven’t made myself throw up. I’ve had moments where I wished I could just undo it somehow, if that makes sense, but I don’t go and vomit on purpose. It’s more like I just sit there feeling awful about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to try to burn off calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I’m not really someone who goes to the gym for hours or anything like that. Sometimes after it happens I feel really restless and uncomfortable in my body, and I might think like, “I should move around” or “I need to do something,” but I don’t have, like, a routine where I’m trying to burn it all off.\n\nI do clean for work, so I’m on my feet a lot anyway, and sometimes I’ll sort of tell myself that being busy or moving around is better than just sitting there feeling disgusting, but I don’t think I’m exercising on purpose to cancel out what I ate. It’s more the guilt and the feeling bad about myself that sits there afterward.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s been pretty regular, I guess. For the past several months, it’s usually at least once a week. It tends to happen at night, after work. I’ll stop and buy way too much food, more than I meant to, and then I just eat it all in one stretch.\n\nI keep thinking maybe it was just a bad week or a stress thing, but when I look back, I can’t really remember a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. Like, it might skip a week, but then it comes back again. So… yeah, usually weekly, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t weigh myself regularly because it kind of makes me spiral, so I don’t have a number to give you. My clothes maybe feel different sometimes, but then other days I convince myself I’m just bloated or I’m overthinking it.\n\nI haven’t had, like, a dramatic change that I can point to for sure. It’s more that I feel uncomfortable in my body and then I start checking or avoiding checking, if that makes sense. So… I don’t really know if my weight has changed significantly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to undo the effects of one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I mean, I know what you mean, like taking something to make yourself go to the bathroom or lose water weight or whatever, but that’s not something I do after those episodes.\n\nI’ve maybe had the thought in a really panicky way, like, “How do I make this not count?” but I don’t go buy laxatives or take water pills or anything like that. Honestly, I’d be kind of scared to mess with that stuff. Afterward I mostly just feel gross and guilty and try to get through it, but I’m not using anything like that to undo it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food in a short period, but you don't feel guilty or ashamed about it afterward?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… if it’s actually one of those episodes, then afterward I pretty much always feel bad about it. That’s kind of the whole reason I finally came in, honestly, because it’s not just eating too much and moving on. It’s like this heavy, gross feeling afterward, like I’m disgusted with myself or really disappointed in myself.\n\nI’ll sit there thinking, “Why couldn’t you just stop?” or “Why don’t you have more control?” And then sometimes I write about it in my journal, but it doesn’t come out helpful or reflective, it comes out really mean. Like I’ll write awful things about myself that I wouldn’t say to another person. So… no, I don’t think there are many times where it happens and I feel fine afterward. The shame part is usually right there with it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by 'at least once a week', is it exactly once a week or are there weeks where it happens more than once?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly once a week, no. I guess when I say “at least once,” I mean there’s usually one night every week where it happens, but some weeks it can be more than that. The pattern I notice most is after work — I’ll stop somewhere or order food, and I end up buying way more than I meant to, and then I eat it all in one stretch once I’m home.\n\nIt’s been like that for the past several months. I’m trying to think if there was a stretch where it really stopped, and… I honestly can’t remember a recent month where I went more than maybe a week or so without it happening. So it’s not like a perfectly scheduled thing, like every Tuesday or something, but it’s frequent enough that it feels like it’s always coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you have weeks where these eating episodes happen more than twice?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are some weeks where it’s more than twice, but I don’t think that’s the usual pattern. Usually it’s more like there’s at least one night in the week where I get off work and I’m already kind of in that mode, and I stop for food or order too much, and then once I’m home I eat it all in one stretch.\n\nBut it’s not like I can say, “Oh, it’s exactly once every week,” because some weeks it does happen more. I’m trying to be accurate, because I don’t want to exaggerate it, but I also don’t want to make it sound smaller than it is. For the past several months, I really can’t think of a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. It keeps coming back, even when I tell myself I’m not going to do it again.\n\nDoctor: During these eating episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the clearest part of it, honestly. Once I start, it feels like I’m not really making decisions one by one anymore. It’s like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically, even if part of my brain is still talking at me the whole time.\n\nLike I’ll literally be thinking, “Fumi, stop. You have sketches to finish. You said you were going to work on them tonight.” Or I’ll think, “Just put the rest away, you don’t need more.” But then I don’t actually do that. I just keep going. It’s not even always that the food tastes amazing by that point, either. Yeah, I think… like I said, not really often. I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to make it sound like I’m never critical of myself, because I definitely can be. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I came in because I’ve been having these eating episodes, and what really scares me is how I feel afterward. Like, after it happens, I just feel so gross and ashamed, and really disappointed in myself. I keep thinking, “Why can’t I just have more control?” and then I sort of spiral.\n\nI’ve been writing in my journal afterward, which I thought would help, but honestly it’s turned into me writing really awful things about myself. Like I’m just sitting there tearing myself apart on paper. And I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels… I don’t know, like proof that something’s wrong with me. I don’t want to keep feeling like that after I eat.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt really sick and uncomfortable afterward, like physically gross and way too full, but I haven’t made myself throw up. I’ve had moments where I wished I could just undo it somehow, if that makes sense, but I don’t go and vomit on purpose. It’s more like I just sit there feeling awful about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to try to burn off calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I’m not really someone who goes to the gym for hours or anything like that. Sometimes after it happens I feel really restless and uncomfortable in my body, and I might think like, “I should move around” or “I need to do something,” but I don’t have, like, a routine where I’m trying to burn it all off.\n\nI do clean for work, so I’m on my feet a lot anyway, and sometimes I’ll sort of tell myself that being busy or moving around is better than just sitting there feeling disgusting, but I don’t think I’m exercising on purpose to cancel out what I ate. It’s more the guilt and the feeling bad about myself that sits there afterward.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s been pretty regular, I guess. For the past several months, it’s usually at least once a week. It tends to happen at night, after work. I’ll stop and buy way too much food, more than I meant to, and then I just eat it all in one stretch.\n\nI keep thinking maybe it was just a bad week or a stress thing, but when I look back, I can’t really remember a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. Like, it might skip a week, but then it comes back again. So… yeah, usually weekly, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t weigh myself regularly because it kind of makes me spiral, so I don’t have a number to give you. My clothes maybe feel different sometimes, but then other days I convince myself I’m just bloated or I’m overthinking it.\n\nI haven’t had, like, a dramatic change that I can point to for sure. It’s more that I feel uncomfortable in my body and then I start checking or avoiding checking, if that makes sense. So… I don’t really know if my weight has changed significantly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to undo the effects of one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I mean, I know what you mean, like taking something to make yourself go to the bathroom or lose water weight or whatever, but that’s not something I do after those episodes.\n\nI’ve maybe had the thought in a really panicky way, like, “How do I make this not count?” but I don’t go buy laxatives or take water pills or anything like that. Honestly, I’d be kind of scared to mess with that stuff. Afterward I mostly just feel gross and guilty and try to get through it, but I’m not using anything like that to undo it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food in a short period, but you don't feel guilty or ashamed about it afterward?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… if it’s actually one of those episodes, then afterward I pretty much always feel bad about it. That’s kind of the whole reason I finally came in, honestly, because it’s not just eating too much and moving on. It’s like this heavy, gross feeling afterward, like I’m disgusted with myself or really disappointed in myself.\n\nI’ll sit there thinking, “Why couldn’t you just stop?” or “Why don’t you have more control?” And then sometimes I write about it in my journal, but it doesn’t come out helpful or reflective, it comes out really mean. Like I’ll write awful things about myself that I wouldn’t say to another person. So… no, I don’t think there are many times where it happens and I feel fine afterward. The shame part is usually right there with it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by 'at least once a week', is it exactly once a week or are there weeks where it happens more than once?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly once a week, no. I guess when I say “at least once,” I mean there’s usually one night every week where it happens, but some weeks it can be more than that. The pattern I notice most is after work — I’ll stop somewhere or order food, and I end up buying way more than I meant to, and then I eat it all in one stretch once I’m home.\n\nIt’s been like that for the past several months. I’m trying to think if there was a stretch where it really stopped, and… I honestly can’t remember a recent month where I went more than maybe a week or so without it happening. So it’s not like a perfectly scheduled thing, like every Tuesday or something, but it’s frequent enough that it feels like it’s always coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you have weeks where these eating episodes happen more than twice?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are some weeks where it’s more than twice, but I don’t think that’s the usual pattern. Usually it’s more like there’s at least one night in the week where I get off work and I’m already kind of in that mode, and I stop for food or order too much, and then once I’m home I eat it all in one stretch.\n\nBut it’s not like I can say, “Oh, it’s exactly once every week,” because some weeks it does happen more. I’m trying to be accurate, because I don’t want to exaggerate it, but I also don’t want to make it sound smaller than it is. For the past several months, I really can’t think of a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. It keeps coming back, even when I tell myself I’m not going to do it again.\n\nDoctor: During these eating episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the clearest part of it, honestly. Once I start, it feels like I’m not really making decisions one by one anymore. It’s like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically, even if part of my brain is still talking at me the whole time.\n\nLike I’ll literally be thinking, “Fumi, stop. You have sketches to finish. You said you were going to work on them tonight.” Or I’ll think, “Just put the rest away, you don’t need more.” But then I don’t actually do that. I just keep going. It’s not even always that the food tastes amazing by that point, either. Sometimes I’m already not enjoying it, but I still keep eating until it’s gone, or until I feel so uncomfortable that stopping kind of happens because I physically don’t want to keep going.\n\nSo yes, it does feel like I can’t stop once I’ve started. Or maybe… I know technically I could stop, but in the moment it really doesn’t feel like I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself or feel guilty about things unrelated to your eating?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I do criticize myself sometimes, like anyone does, I guess — if I mess something up at work or if I procrastinate on something I meant to do. But I don’t know if I’d say it’s a big thing outside of the eating stuff.\n\nThe guilt is mostly tied to that, at least that’s what stands out to me. Like afterward I can get really harsh with myself. But unrelated to eating… I’d have to think about it more. Nothing is immediately coming to mind where I’m constantly feeling guilty about everything, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being very critical of yourself in situations that have nothing to do with food or eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not often, at least. It’s like this heavy, gross feeling afterward, like I’m disgusted with myself or really disappointed in myself.\n\nI’ll sit there thinking, “Why couldn’t you just stop?” or “Why don’t you have more control?” And then sometimes I write about it in my journal, but it doesn’t come out helpful or reflective, it comes out really mean. Like I’ll write awful things about myself that I wouldn’t say to another person. So… no, I don’t think there are many times where it happens and I feel fine afterward. The shame part is usually right there with it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by 'at least once a week', is it exactly once a week or are there weeks where it happens more than once?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly once a week, no. I guess when I say “at least once,” I mean there’s usually one night every week where it happens, but some weeks it can be more than that. The pattern I notice most is after work — I’ll stop somewhere or order food, and I end up buying way more than I meant to, and then I eat it all in one stretch once I’m home.\n\nIt’s been like that for the past several months. I’m trying to think if there was a stretch where it really stopped, and… I honestly can’t remember a recent month where I went more than maybe a week or so without it happening. So it’s not like a perfectly scheduled thing, like every Tuesday or something, but it’s frequent enough that it feels like it’s always coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you have weeks where these eating episodes happen more than twice?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are some weeks where it’s more than twice, but I don’t think that’s the usual pattern. Usually it’s more like there’s at least one night in the week where I get off work and I’m already kind of in that mode, and I stop for food or order too much, and then once I’m home I eat it all in one stretch.\n\nBut it’s not like I can say, “Oh, it’s exactly once every week,” because some weeks it does happen more. I’m trying to be accurate, because I don’t want to exaggerate it, but I also don’t want to make it sound smaller than it is. For the past several months, I really can’t think of a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. It keeps coming back, even when I tell myself I’m not going to do it again.\n\nDoctor: During these eating episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the clearest part of it, honestly. Once I start, it feels like I’m not really making decisions one by one anymore. It’s like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically, even if part of my brain is still talking at me the whole time.\n\nLike I’ll literally be thinking, “Fumi, stop. You have sketches to finish. You said you were going to work on them tonight.” Or I’ll think, “Just put the rest away, you don’t need more.” But then I don’t actually do that. I just keep going. It’s not even always that the food tastes amazing by that point, either. Sometimes I’m already not enjoying it, but I still keep eating until it’s gone, or until I feel so uncomfortable that stopping kind of happens because I physically don’t want to keep going.\n\nSo yes, it does feel like I can’t stop once I’ve started. Or maybe… I know technically I could stop, but in the moment it really doesn’t feel like I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself or feel guilty about things unrelated to your eating?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I do criticize myself sometimes, like anyone does, I guess — if I mess something up at work or if I procrastinate on something I meant to do. But I don’t know if I’d say it’s a big thing outside of the eating stuff.\n\nThe guilt is mostly tied to that, at least that’s what stands out to me. Like afterward I can get really harsh with myself. But unrelated to eating… I’d have to think about it more. Nothing is immediately coming to mind where I’m constantly feeling guilty about everything, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being very critical of yourself in situations that have nothing to do with food or eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not often, at least. I mean, I can be hard on myself in a normal way, like if I’m late getting something done or I feel like I handled something awkwardly, I’ll replay it a bit. But when you say “very critical,” I think of that really intense kind of beating myself up, and that’s much more connected to the eating for me.\n\nOutside of food, I don’t walk around constantly thinking I’m a terrible person or anything like that. I might get annoyed with myself or embarrassed, but it usually passes. So… no, I wouldn’t say I often do that in situations that have nothing to do with food.\n\nDoctor: Is the harsh self-criticism you mentioned mostly limited to after these eating episodes or does it also happen frequently in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly after the eating episodes. That’s really the part that made me come in, honestly, because afterward I just feel… gross, and disappointed in myself, like I should have had more control or I should have stopped earlier. It feels very personal, like it turns into “what is wrong with me?” instead of just “I ate too much.”\n\nAnd I’ll write about it in my journal, which sounds like it should help, but sometimes it’s just awful. I’ll write really harsh things about myself, things I probably wouldn’t say to another person. Like I’ll sit there feeling sick and embarrassed and write pages of how I messed up again and how I should be better than this.\n\nIn other areas of my life, I can criticize myself, but it doesn’t usually get that intense or mean. It’s really after those episodes that it kind of spirals into that ugly self-talk.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being very critical of yourself in situations that are not related to eating or food?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think… like I said, not really often. I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to make it sound like I’m never critical of myself, because I definitely can be. Like if I’m at work and I forget something or I feel like I didn’t do a good enough job, I might think, “Ugh, why did I do that?” or I’ll feel embarrassed for a while.\n\nBut the really harsh stuff — the kind where I’m just tearing myself apart — I don’t notice that happening much when it has nothing to do with food. It’s not like I’m constantly walking around judging every part of myself all day. It’s more… specific. Outside of eating, it’s usually more like frustration or embarrassment, and then it fades. So I’d say no, not often in other situations.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. For the past several months, I really can’t think of a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. Nothing is immediately coming to mind where I’m constantly feeling guilty about everything, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being very critical of yourself in situations that have nothing to do with food or eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not often, at least. I mean, I can be hard on myself in a normal way, like if I’m late getting something done or I feel like I handled something awkwardly, I’ll replay it a bit. But when you say “very critical,” I think of that really intense kind of beating myself up, and that’s much more connected to the eating for me.\n\nOutside of food, I don’t walk around constantly thinking I’m a terrible person or anything like that. I might get annoyed with myself or embarrassed, but it usually passes. So… no, I wouldn’t say I often do that in situations that have nothing to do with food.\n\nDoctor: Is the harsh self-criticism you mentioned mostly limited to after these eating episodes or does it also happen frequently in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly after the eating episodes. Like I’ll sit there feeling sick and embarrassed and write pages of how I messed up again and how I should be better than this.\n\nIn other areas of my life, I can criticize myself, but it doesn’t usually get that intense or mean. It’s really after those episodes that it kind of spirals into that ugly self-talk.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being very critical of yourself in situations that are not related to eating or food?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think… like I said, not really often. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve been thinking about that as you’ve been asking, because I don’t want to just automatically say “no” if there are things I’m missing, but… it really doesn’t feel like it’s across my whole life.

Like, I can be hard on myself in the normal ways, I guess — if I make a mistake or I feel like I handled something awkwardly, I might beat myself up a little in the moment. But it’s not usually this harsh, nasty voice that sticks around or takes over. The really intense criticism is much more tied to the eating stuff.

So I wouldn’t say it’s many areas of my life. It’s more like… I have moments of being self-critical elsewhere, but the kind that feels really severe is mostly around that one area.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I came in because I’ve been having these eating episodes, and what really scares me is how I feel afterward. Like, after it happens, I just feel so gross and ashamed, and really disappointed in myself. I keep thinking, “Why can’t I just have more control?” and then I sort of spiral.\n\nI’ve been writing in my journal afterward, which I thought would help, but honestly it’s turned into me writing really awful things about myself. Like I’m just sitting there tearing myself apart on paper. And I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels… I don’t know, like proof that something’s wrong with me. I don’t want to keep feeling like that after I eat.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt really sick and uncomfortable afterward, like physically gross and way too full, but I haven’t made myself throw up. I’ve had moments where I wished I could just undo it somehow, if that makes sense, but I don’t go and vomit on purpose. It’s more like I just sit there feeling awful about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to try to burn off calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I’m not really someone who goes to the gym for hours or anything like that. Sometimes after it happens I feel really restless and uncomfortable in my body, and I might think like, “I should move around” or “I need to do something,” but I don’t have, like, a routine where I’m trying to burn it all off.\n\nI do clean for work, so I’m on my feet a lot anyway, and sometimes I’ll sort of tell myself that being busy or moving around is better than just sitting there feeling disgusting, but I don’t think I’m exercising on purpose to cancel out what I ate. It’s more the guilt and the feeling bad about myself that sits there afterward.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s been pretty regular, I guess. For the past several months, it’s usually at least once a week. It tends to happen at night, after work. I’ll stop and buy way too much food, more than I meant to, and then I just eat it all in one stretch.\n\nI keep thinking maybe it was just a bad week or a stress thing, but when I look back, I can’t really remember a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. Like, it might skip a week, but then it comes back again. So… yeah, usually weekly, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t weigh myself regularly because it kind of makes me spiral, so I don’t have a number to give you. My clothes maybe feel different sometimes, but then other days I convince myself I’m just bloated or I’m overthinking it.\n\nI haven’t had, like, a dramatic change that I can point to for sure. It’s more that I feel uncomfortable in my body and then I start checking or avoiding checking, if that makes sense. So… I don’t really know if my weight has changed significantly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to undo the effects of one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I mean, I know what you mean, like taking something to make yourself go to the bathroom or lose water weight or whatever, but that’s not something I do after those episodes.\n\nI’ve maybe had the thought in a really panicky way, like, “How do I make this not count?” but I don’t go buy laxatives or take water pills or anything like that. Honestly, I’d be kind of scared to mess with that stuff. Afterward I mostly just feel gross and guilty and try to get through it, but I’m not using anything like that to undo it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are times when you eat a large amount of food in a short period, but you don't feel guilty or ashamed about it afterward?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… if it’s actually one of those episodes, then afterward I pretty much always feel bad about it. That’s kind of the whole reason I finally came in, honestly, because it’s not just eating too much and moving on. It’s like this heavy, gross feeling afterward, like I’m disgusted with myself or really disappointed in myself.\n\nI’ll sit there thinking, “Why couldn’t you just stop?” or “Why don’t you have more control?” And then sometimes I write about it in my journal, but it doesn’t come out helpful or reflective, it comes out really mean. Like I’ll write awful things about myself that I wouldn’t say to another person. So… no, I don’t think there are many times where it happens and I feel fine afterward. The shame part is usually right there with it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by 'at least once a week', is it exactly once a week or are there weeks where it happens more than once?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly once a week, no. I guess when I say “at least once,” I mean there’s usually one night every week where it happens, but some weeks it can be more than that. The pattern I notice most is after work — I’ll stop somewhere or order food, and I end up buying way more than I meant to, and then I eat it all in one stretch once I’m home.\n\nIt’s been like that for the past several months. I’m trying to think if there was a stretch where it really stopped, and… I honestly can’t remember a recent month where I went more than maybe a week or so without it happening. So it’s not like a perfectly scheduled thing, like every Tuesday or something, but it’s frequent enough that it feels like it’s always coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you have weeks where these eating episodes happen more than twice?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are some weeks where it’s more than twice, but I don’t think that’s the usual pattern. Usually it’s more like there’s at least one night in the week where I get off work and I’m already kind of in that mode, and I stop for food or order too much, and then once I’m home I eat it all in one stretch.\n\nBut it’s not like I can say, “Oh, it’s exactly once every week,” because some weeks it does happen more. I’m trying to be accurate, because I don’t want to exaggerate it, but I also don’t want to make it sound smaller than it is. For the past several months, I really can’t think of a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. It keeps coming back, even when I tell myself I’m not going to do it again.\n\nDoctor: During these eating episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the clearest part of it, honestly. Once I start, it feels like I’m not really making decisions one by one anymore. It’s like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically, even if part of my brain is still talking at me the whole time.\n\nLike I’ll literally be thinking, “Fumi, stop. You have sketches to finish. You said you were going to work on them tonight.” Or I’ll think, “Just put the rest away, you don’t need more.” But then I don’t actually do that. I just keep going. It’s not even always that the food tastes amazing by that point, either. Sometimes I’m already not enjoying it, but I still keep eating until it’s gone, or until I feel so uncomfortable that stopping kind of happens because I physically don’t want to keep going.\n\nSo yes, it does feel like I can’t stop once I’ve started. Or maybe… I know technically I could stop, but in the moment it really doesn’t feel like I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself or feel guilty about things unrelated to your eating?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I do criticize myself sometimes, like anyone does, I guess — if I mess something up at work or if I procrastinate on something I meant to do. But I don’t know if I’d say it’s a big thing outside of the eating stuff.\n\nThe guilt is mostly tied to that, at least that’s what stands out to me. Like afterward I can get really harsh with myself. But unrelated to eating… I’d have to think about it more. Nothing is immediately coming to mind where I’m constantly feeling guilty about everything, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being very critical of yourself in situations that have nothing to do with food or eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not often, at least. I mean, I can be hard on myself in a normal way, like if I’m late getting something done or I feel like I handled something awkwardly, I’ll replay it a bit. But when you say “very critical,” I think of that really intense kind of beating myself up, and that’s much more connected to the eating for me.\n\nOutside of food, I don’t walk around constantly thinking I’m a terrible person or anything like that. I might get annoyed with myself or embarrassed, but it usually passes. So… no, I wouldn’t say I often do that in situations that have nothing to do with food.\n\nDoctor: Is the harsh self-criticism you mentioned mostly limited to after these eating episodes or does it also happen frequently in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly after the eating episodes. That’s really the part that made me come in, honestly, because afterward I just feel… gross, and disappointed in myself, like I should have had more control or I should have stopped earlier. It feels very personal, like it turns into “what is wrong with me?” instead of just “I ate too much.”\n\nAnd I’ll write about it in my journal, which sounds like it should help, but sometimes it’s just awful. I’ll write really harsh things about myself, things I probably wouldn’t say to another person. Like I’ll sit there feeling sick and embarrassed and write pages of how I messed up again and how I should be better than this.\n\nIn other areas of my life, I can criticize myself, but it doesn’t usually get that intense or mean. It’s really after those episodes that it kind of spirals into that ugly self-talk.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being very critical of yourself in situations that are not related to eating or food?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think… like I said, not really often. I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to make it sound like I’m never critical of myself, because I definitely can be. Like if I’m at work and I forget something or I feel like I didn’t do a good enough job, I might think, “Ugh, why did I do that?” or I’ll feel embarrassed for a while.\n\nBut the really harsh stuff — the kind where I’m just tearing myself apart — I don’t notice that happening much when it has nothing to do with food. It’s not like I’m constantly walking around judging every part of myself all day. It’s more… specific. Outside of eating, it’s usually more like frustration or embarrassment, and then it fades. So I’d say no, not often in other situations.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself being very critical of yourself in situations that have nothing to do with food or eating?\n\nPatient: I mean… not very often, I don’t think. I’m trying to answer that honestly because I don’t want to make it sound like I’m super confident all the time or something. I do get irritated with myself, like if I mess up at work or I say something awkward and then later I’m like, “Why did I say it like that?” I can definitely replay things in my head.\n\nBut when you say “very critical,” like really harsh or mean toward myself, that’s not something I notice happening a lot outside of the food stuff. In regular life it’s more like embarrassment or frustration, and then it usually goes away after a bit. It doesn’t usually turn into this whole intense thing where I’m just tearing myself down.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be… rarely, or not often.
